Meta-omschrijving (149 tekens)The Dutch Algorithm Register lists 1,613 algorithms, yet government often does not know if AI works. What is happening, and what does the AI Act ask?
2105 woorden3 interne links0 plekken [RICHARD]slug: /en/ai-in-government-netherlands/concept-toets: okkoopvraag: geen eigen GEO-metingnulmeting: Search Console 90 dagen vóór publicatie (zie zoekwoord)tegenhanger: NL-versie bestaat als concept (ai-bij-de-overheid); inhoudelijk gelijk, in Brits Engels voor een internationale lezer; hreflang koppelen bij publicatie

AI in government: what Dutch municipalities and central government are doing now, and what the AI Act asks

Richard van Hooijdonk · datum volgt · concept

Dutch government is using more and more AI, mostly for internal processes, with civil servants as the users. The Dutch Algorithm Register listed 1,613 algorithms on 31 October 2026. Yet according to the Netherlands Court of Audit, for 35 percent of central government's AI systems in use it was unknown whether they did what they should.

Picture a municipal social support team in the Netherlands. In Almere, the Algorithm Register lists an AI agent meant to draft the assessment report for an application for support at home, after which the consultant checks everything. According to the registration, the agent is temporarily on hold for technical reasons. Much government AI looks like this: support for civil servants in their own work. At the same time, Dutch government has a hard lesson behind it: the childcare benefits scandal showed how badly things can go wrong when a system judges people wrongly. And the European AI legislation (the AI Act) sets extra requirements for public authorities.

Below, we stick to what the Dutch central government, the Association of Netherlands Municipalities (VNG), the regulators and the European Commission write themselves. My expectation: in government, the technology is not the hardest part. What matters is that civil servants are open to AI and at the same time stay sharp on what it means for residents.

Dutch government and AI today

Mostly internal work: around two thirds of central government's AI systems are aimed at internal processes.

In October 2024, the Netherlands Court of Audit (Algemene Rekenkamer) mapped for the first time how much AI central government uses. In the study Focus on AI in central government, 70 government organisations together reported 433 AI systems. 167 of them were still ongoing experiments. Around two thirds of the systems are aimed at internal processes without a direct impact on citizens and businesses, such as turning conversations into text or anonymising documents.

Generative AI is on the rise too, as far as the figures can show. According to the Government-wide Monitor on Generative AI, which the research organisation TNO published in December 2025 for the Ministry of the Interior and Kingdom Relations (BZK), the number of applications found rose from 8 in June 2024 to 81 in June 2025. Of these, 37 were in use and 29 were still an experiment. In more than 75 percent of the applications, civil servants are the users, not citizens. TNO cannot establish how representative this picture is.

Government on generative AI

Since April 2025, civil servants have more room to use generative AI, under conditions.

In January 2024, the Dutch cabinet presented a government-wide vision on generative AI. It sets out four principles: generative AI must be safe and fair, serve human well-being and human autonomy, and contribute to sustainability and prosperity. One of the six lines of action: government experiments with generative AI itself, safely.

In April 2025, a government-wide position followed, agreed with municipalities, provinces, water authorities and implementing agencies. It gave civil servants more room to use generative AI in their work, for example to speed up permit applications or answer questions from citizens faster. But under conditions, such as risk analyses. Central government also encourages applications developed in Europe and open-source solutions. According to the Dutch government, these are the first rules for AI that apply to the whole of government.

The Dutch Algorithm Register

The register is growing fast, but registration is not yet a legal requirement.

In the Algorithm Register (Algoritmeregister), Dutch public authorities publish information about the algorithms they use. The register focuses on algorithms with a high impact, including high-risk AI. According to a letter to the Dutch House of Representatives of 9 September 2025, it then held 1,000 algorithms, against 675 on 1 January 2025. On 31 October 2026, the register counted 1,613. Registration is not yet a legal requirement. In December 2022, the cabinet did announce a legal obligation of transparency about algorithms, but in September 2025 the State Secretary for the Interior wrote that he did not (yet) consider such an obligation opportune at this time.

Three recent examples from the register show what it looks like:

  • Wmo Agent, municipality of Almere: AI writes a draft of the assessment report for an application under the Social Support Act (Wmo), the Dutch law on care and support at home. The agent does not decide on support; the consultant checks everything and a colleague reviews the report. According to the registration, the agent has been put on hold temporarily for technical reasons.
  • AI desk, social services department of Noardwest Fryslân: staff query a knowledge base on topics such as the Participation Act and the Social Support Act. A staff member always checks the answer, and the AI desk never talks to residents directly.
  • Service desk assistant, province of Noord-Holland: AI drafts answers to questions from residents and businesses to the provincial service desk.

How do municipalities use AI?

In the TNO monitor, municipalities are the largest group, and the VNG focuses on working together.

Of the 81 generative AI applications in the TNO monitor, 34 were at municipalities, mostly chatbots for civil servants and for contact with residents. One example is GEM, a virtual assistant on the websites of 25 municipalities, started in 2019 by Utrecht and Tilburg. The municipalities share the costs and a single privacy assessment (DPIA, data protection impact assessment).

The VNG, the Association of Netherlands Municipalities, focuses on working together. Two guides appeared in October 2026. The guide to scaling up AI (27 October) helps public authorities roll out a successful pilot across more organisations. The guide on AI in software updates (8 October) helps buyers now that municipalities increasingly receive software updates with AI built in.

What does the AI Act ask?

The AI Act sets extra requirements, above all for AI that assesses eligibility for essential public assistance benefits and services.

The AI Act affects public authorities on a few extra points. We follow the consolidated text on the European Commission's AI Act Service Desk, after the amendment by the Digital Omnibus.

  • Already in force: since 2 February 2025, the prohibitions apply, such as social scoring that leads to unjustified detrimental treatment of people. Organisations must also ensure the AI literacy of their staff.
  • High risk: AI that a public authority uses to evaluate whether someone is eligible for essential public assistance benefits and services, including healthcare services, or to grant, reduce, revoke or reclaim them, is listed as high risk in Annex III. The strict rules for these systems apply from 2 December 2027.
  • Fundamental rights assessment: before using such a system, public authorities must in principle carry out an assessment of the impact on fundamental rights.
  • Registration: a public authority may not use a high-risk system that is not registered in the EU database (Article 26). For critical infrastructure (Annex III, point 2), registration takes place at national level instead (Article 49).
  • Existing systems: high-risk systems intended for use by public authorities must comply by 2 August 2030 at the latest (Article 111).

Where does it get stuck?

It gets stuck on knowing whether AI works, on knowledge and resources, and on trust.

Knowing whether it works. According to the Court of Audit, for 35 percent of the AI systems central government had in use, it was unknown whether they met expectations. For more than half of the systems, the opportunities had not yet been weighed against the risks. Only 5 percent of the systems were in the register at the time.

Knowledge and resources. In TNO's interviews, public authorities name, among other things, limited AI literacy, data that is not always in order, unclear rules and too little budget and time. Staff also sometimes use AI tools on their own initiative.

Trust. The Dutch childcare benefits scandal shows what is at stake. In December 2021, the Dutch Data Protection Authority (Autoriteit Persoonsgegevens, AP) fined the Dutch Tax and Customs Administration 2.75 million euros. The tax authority used the nationality of applicants for childcare benefit in, among other things, a system that automatically flagged applications as risky. According to the AP, this was unlawful and discriminatory. We wrote earlier about algorithms gone wrong.

How does a public body start?

Start with internal work, set the goal in advance and keep a human in charge.

  1. Start with internal work. According to the Court of Audit, around two thirds of the AI systems are aimed at internal processes, and according to TNO a staff member is the user in more than 75 percent of the generative AI applications. Then map process by process, from a Wmo report to answering questions from residents, which tasks AI can prepare and which decision stays with the civil servant.
  2. Set the goal in advance. The Court of Audit found that it remains unknown whether AI works if the goal has not been set beforehand.
  3. Keep a human in charge. The examples from the register show how: the AI writes a draft, a staff member checks and decides.
  4. Check whether it is high risk. If the system assesses eligibility for essential public assistance benefits and services, for example (Annex III), it is in principle high-risk: stricter rules apply from 2 December 2027, and as a rule a fundamental rights assessment. An exception applies if the system only performs a narrow procedural or preparatory task without materially influencing the decision (Article 6(3)).
  5. Register. Put high-impact algorithms in the register, even though that is not yet mandatory.
  6. Teach your people to work with it. AI literacy has been an obligation since February 2025.
  7. Work together. Build on existing solutions and on the VNG guides, instead of building everything yourself.

The government of the future

The number of applications found is growing fast, but whether AI works and is fair remains an open question.

According to TNO, many generative AI applications are already in use, and the VNG is now helping to scale up pilots. The number of registrations and applications found is growing fast. At the same time, the questions of the Court of Audit and the lesson of the childcare benefits scandal have not yet been answered: does it work, and is it fair to citizens? For high-risk systems in Annex III of the AI Act, such as systems that assess eligibility for essential public assistance benefits and services, stricter requirements apply from 2 December 2027. Before using them, public authorities must in principle also assess the impact on fundamental rights. An ethical perspective belongs there from the start.

AI is not the first new technology in government, and certainly not the last. My advice: let people play and experiment with it themselves, in a safe environment and on a real problem of residents. That is how you learn how it works. It takes leaders who activate their civil servants and give them room, and who then look critically at the results together. Enthusiasm for change and a critical eye suit a government that wants to win back trust. That side of change, the people and the organisation, is the subject of the keynote on the organisation of the future.

Frequently asked questions

What is the Dutch Algorithm Register?

A public government website, algoritmes.overheid.nl, where Dutch public authorities describe which algorithms they use, for what and with what oversight. The register focuses on algorithms with a high impact. On 31 October 2026, it held 1,613 algorithms. Registration is not yet a legal requirement.

Can civil servants use ChatGPT?

Since the government-wide position of April 2025, civil servants have more room to use generative AI, under conditions such as a risk analysis and reliable models. Some organisations offer their own assistant, such as the PZH assistant of the province of Zuid-Holland, only for their own civil servants.

When do the AI Act rules apply?

The prohibitions and the AI literacy obligation have applied since 2 February 2025. For high-risk AI in Annex III, such as AI that assesses eligibility for essential public assistance benefits and services, the strict rules apply from 2 December 2027. Existing high-risk systems intended for public authorities must comply by 2 August 2030 at the latest.

What is the lesson of the benefits scandal?

That a system which automatically flags people as a risk can discriminate if it uses the wrong data. The Dutch Data Protection Authority fined the tax authority for this in 2021.

Richard van Hooijdonk is a trend watcher, futurist and keynote speaker on AI and technology, new leadership and the organisation of the future.

SEO-titel (56 tekens)How much does an AI keynote speaker cost? Fees explained
Meta-omschrijving (153 tekens)What does an AI keynote cost for a European event? What bureaus say about fees, what drives the price, which extra costs apply and how to compare quotes.
1928 woorden4 interne links0 plekken [RICHARD]slug: /en/how-much-does-an-ai-keynote-speaker-cost/concept-toets: okkoopvraag: N23 voorgesteld (vraag 79, docs/voorstel-koopvragen-en-2026-10.md); tot het akkoord meet N7 alleen de NL-prijsvraagnulmeting: nog niet gemeten voor een Engelse prijsvraag; Search Console 90 dagen vóór publicatie (zie zoekwoord), en bij een nieuwe EN-prijskoopvraag een eerste meting in ChatGPT, Perplexity en Geminitegenhanger: NL-versie bestaat als concept (wat-kost-een-keynotespreker-over-ai); geen letterlijke vertaling maar een eigen Engelse versie voor Europese opdrachtgevers; hreflang koppelen bij publicatie

How much does an AI keynote speaker cost? What drives the fee and what you get

Richard van Hooijdonk · datum volgt · concept

No single price applies to every speaker and every event. On the pages checked for this article, none of six English-language speaker bureaus publishes a fee range. According to Chartwell Speakers, fees depend on the speaker's profile and demand, session length, preparation, travel and recording rights. Champions Speakers says a fee normally covers a 30-45 minute keynote plus Q&A.

You are budgeting an event in Europe and want a keynote on AI in the programme. Then you want to know what bureaus themselves say about fees, which factors drive a price and which questions to ask with every quote.

What does an AI keynote cost?

The bureau pages we checked give no general fee range, so you need a quote per speaker.

Many speakers and bureaus do not publish a fixed price. We looked at the websites of six English-language speaker bureaus; for Thinking Heads, only the English home page. This is what they wrote on 24 October 2026:

| Bureau | What the bureau itself says about fees | |---|---| | Speakers Corner | No amounts. Fees "vary widely" depending on the speaker's experience, profile and availability, and on the format and location of the event. An account manager helps you see what is realistic within your budget. | | Chartwell Speakers | No amounts. Fees are shaped by the speaker's profile and demand, the format, the time commitment and the scope of the engagement, including preparation, travel, exclusivity and recording permissions. | | Champions Speakers | No amounts. You get a quote on price and availability for the speaker you choose. The fee normally covers a keynote plus Q&A or a meet and greet; travel and accommodation are separate. | | BigSpeak | No general range. You pay no fees for the bureau's services; the speaker pays the bureau a commission. | | London Speaker Bureau | No fee range for speakers. The enquiry form asks you to choose a budget band, so the bureau can suggest realistic options. | | Thinking Heads | No fee information found on the English home page. |

None of these bureaus names a separate price level for speakers on AI. What counts most is how experienced and well known someone is, and what you ask for. A short online session for one team is different from a tailored keynote for a conference with a thousand guests.

What determines the fee?

Profile, session length, preparation, travel, format and recordings all shape the fee.

Speakers Corner names the speaker's experience, profile and availability, plus the format and location of the event. Chartwell Speakers adds session length, preparation time, travel logistics, exclusivity requests and the use of recordings. Chartwell also writes that time-sensitive bookings, peak dates and international travel can affect availability and sometimes the overall cost. That is why many speakers say "price on request". Below are the factors you can influence yourself.

How long is the keynote?

Bureaus and speakers often offer a few lengths. Champions Speakers writes that a fee normally covers a keynote of 30-45 minutes, followed by Q&A or a meet and greet. On Richard van Hooijdonk's site, a keynote lasts 30, 45 or 60 minutes or longer, with time for questions. A longer session, a second round or an extra workshop on the same day changes the price. Agree in advance how much time there is for questions, because that time is part of the session.

How much tailoring do you need?

An existing talk takes less preparation than one built around your sector, your strategy or your audience. With AI this matters more, because examples date quickly. Speakers Corner writes that most speakers tailor their content to the audience and the event, but that the level of customisation varies by speaker, topic and format. Ask what is included as standard: a briefing call, examples from your own sector, a Q&A or a conversation with the host afterwards.

How far does the speaker travel?

The location determines travel time and sometimes an overnight stay. For international engagements, Chartwell Speakers writes that clients cover approved travel, accommodation and reasonable related expenses, such as flights, ground transport, hotel, meals and visa or entry costs where they apply. Champions Speakers also lists travel and accommodation as separate from the fee. BigSpeak, a bureau based in the United States, writes that most speakers now include travel in their fee. So always ask how travel is handled in your quote.

Which language does the speaker use?

At a European event with guests from several countries, English is often the shared language. But not every speaker is equally strong in English and in the local language. Ask whether the fee differs by language, and whether the speaker has given this session in that language before.

Online or on location?

An online session saves travel time and travel costs. On the other hand, a full room calls for a different kind of session than a webinar, and an online session needs its own preparation. Chartwell Speakers recommends a short technical check for virtual and hybrid events, and writes that the client is responsible for the quality of the connection. For Richard van Hooijdonk, for example, the fee for an international keynote is set per event.

Do you want to record or stream it?

Recording is not automatically part of the deal. Chartwell Speakers writes that filming, livestreaming or reusing any part of a presentation needs written approval in advance, and that usage terms vary by speaker. Richard van Hooijdonk's page for organisers also asks you to arrange filming, streaming or sharing the presentation in advance. Agree on it before you sign, and put it in the quote.

Do you pay more via a bureau?

Several bureaus say you pay them no separate fee; whether the total is the same, you have to check.

BigSpeak writes that you pay no fees for its services, because the speakers pay the bureau a commission. Champions Speakers puts it the same way: "you won't pay us - the speaker pays us". BigSpeak also explains that some bureaus charge up to 30 percent of a keynote speaker's fee, but that for companies this commission is often included in the speaker's flat rate. For a speaker who is not on its own roster, BigSpeak writes that it books "at the same fee as any other bureau". So at BigSpeak and Champions Speakers you pay no separate bureau fee, but that does not prove that booking directly would cost the same in total.

Whether you book through a bureau or directly depends mainly on what you need. A bureau helps if you don't have a name yet or want to compare several speakers. Speakers Corner and Champions Speakers both mention help with finding a replacement if a speaker has to cancel. If you already know who you want, you can also contact the speaker directly. In both cases, ask for the same items in the quote, so you can put them side by side.

Which extra costs come on top?

Travel, accommodation, visa costs, technology, recording rights and VAT can all come on top.

Check which of these items are in the quote and which come on top:

  • Travel: flights, trains or ground transport. Chartwell Speakers and Champions Speakers list these as separate costs.
  • Accommodation and meals, if an overnight stay is needed.
  • Visa or entry costs, if they apply (Chartwell Speakers).
  • Technology: sound, microphone, screen and a stable connection for online sessions.
  • Recording and usage rights, if you want to film, stream or share the session.
  • VAT. Ask whether the amount in the quote includes or excludes VAT. For a speaker from another EU country, ask your finance team how VAT is handled for your booking.

What do you get for the money?

Ask whether a briefing call, tailoring, Q&A and a replacement in case of illness are included.

For the price of a keynote, you buy more than an hour on stage. Ask which of these are included:

  • A briefing call about your goal, your audience and your programme. Speakers Corner writes that it always aims to organise a call between the speaker and the client.
  • Tailoring: examples from your sector and a link to the theme of the day.
  • A current talk. On AI, a talk dates quickly; ask when it was last updated.
  • Room for questions from the audience, or a conversation with the host.
  • Agreements on recording and on sharing the slides afterwards. Speakers Corner notes that speakers do not always share their slides in advance.
  • A replacement in case of illness or emergency. Speakers Corner and Champions Speakers both write that they look for a replacement; ask how this works for your booking.

From a keynote on AI you can also expect the audience to know where to start afterwards: with their own processes, asking for each task what AI can do and what stays with people. You can read more about what a guest speaker can add to an event in The impact of a guest speaker.

Richard van Hooijdonk's fees

Fees for Richard van Hooijdonk's international keynotes are on request and set per event.

On the page about Richard van Hooijdonk, it says: "Fees for international keynotes are on request." A keynote lasts 30, 45 or 60 minutes or longer, in Dutch or English, in person or online. You send your date and a short description of your event and target audience, and the proposal gives the topic, format, length and fee for your event, so you can talk it over with your colleagues. Want to know what a keynote at your event would cost? Request a proposal via the speaker kit. One example of such a keynote is The AI Revolution.

How do you compare quotes?

Send every speaker the same brief and compare on what you get, not only on the amount.

  1. Send the same brief to every speaker or bureau: date, city, length, language, format and audience. Chartwell Speakers asks for date, location, format, audience type and size, theme, session goals and budget range.
  2. Ask whether the amount includes VAT and which extra costs will follow, such as travel and accommodation.
  3. Ask what the fee covers: briefing call, tailoring, Q&A, recording.
  4. Ask about cancellation and replacement in case of illness or emergency.
  5. Book in good time. Speakers Corner writes that starting three to six months in advance can help for major conferences or high-profile speakers. Champions Speakers advises 6-12 months for a big name and recommends booking more than a month ahead.
  6. Compare on what you get, not only on the amount. A general guide to booking any speaker is Hire the perfect speaker. Still unsure which speaker fits? Use the checklist How to choose an AI keynote speaker: 8 questions to ask first.

Frequently asked questions

What does an AI speaker cost on average?

We found no published average on the bureau pages we checked. Ask for a quote for the speaker you have in mind.

Is an online keynote cheaper?

It often saves travel time and travel costs, but there is no fixed rule. For Richard van Hooijdonk, fees for international keynotes are on request, whether online or on location.

Can you record a keynote?

Only with the speaker's permission. Chartwell Speakers writes that recording and livestreaming need written approval in advance and that usage terms vary by speaker. Agree on it beforehand and put it in the quote.

Richard van Hooijdonk is a trend watcher, futurist and keynote speaker on AI and technology, new leadership and the organisation of the future.

Choosing a keynote speaker on AI and the future of healthcare: 7 questions for your European conference

Richard van Hooijdonk · datum volgt · concept

A keynote speaker on AI in healthcare is chosen on what your audience should do differently afterwards, not on a name. A board, a medical team and IT each need their own story: according to the European Commission the EU AI Act became applicable on 2 August 2026. Below: seven questions, one scorecard and the sources.

You are organising a healthcare conference in Europe, perhaps for hospital boards, insurers, a medical association or a technology supplier. Everyone wants AI on the programme, but a list of speaker names does not tell you who fits your room. This guide gives you the questions, a way to compare the answers and the facts for the briefing.

Why a healthcare specialist?

Healthcare has its own regulators, its own data and professionals who carry responsibility for patients.

AI touches the consulting room, the ward and the planning office at the same time, and the regulators treat it accordingly. The European Commission lists AI as a safety component of a product, with an AI application in robot-assisted surgery as its example, among the high-risk cases under the AI Act. The European Medicines Agency adopted a reflection paper on the use of AI in the medicinal product lifecycle in September 2024, with considerations for developers who want to use AI and machine learning in a safe and effective way at the different stages of a medicine lifecycle.

Health systems are also at different stages. On 19 November 2025 WHO/Europe published the first assessment of AI in health systems across the European Region, drawing on insights from 50 Member States. A speaker who knows that landscape can place your country and your organisation in it, instead of giving a generic AI talk.

Which 7 questions do you ask?

Seven questions, from the audience and the goal to the rules and the fee.

1. Who is in the room, what should change?

Decide on one main audience: the board, clinicians, or the data and IT teams. Ask the speaker how the story changes for each of them, and what the audience should start, stop or decide after the session.

2. Does the speaker know the European rules?

Ask when the AI Act applies to your use cases. The Commission's own page says the Act entered into force on 1 August 2024 and became applicable on 2 August 2026, with some exceptions; prohibited practices and AI literacy obligations have applied since 2 February 2025, the rules for general-purpose AI models since 2 August 2025, and the rules for AI in regulated products under Annex I apply from 2 August 2028. A speaker who gets these dates wrong is likely to get the rest wrong too.

3. Recent healthcare examples, with sources?

Ask for three concrete examples with the organisation, the year and the source. For background on the topics, see our articles on generative AI in healthcare and hospital at home.

4. Organisation as well as technology?

Ask how much of the keynote goes to work processes, responsibility, data agreements and people, and how much to the technology itself.

5. How are risk and ethics handled?

Bias, errors, liability and patient trust belong in the story. The WHO has published guidance on one type of generative AI, large multi-modal models, and writes that wide use and application has been predicted in health care, scientific research, public health and drug development. Ask how the risks come up in the keynote and what the audience can do about them.

6. Is the keynote tailored to your programme?

Ask what the speaker needs from you (programme, audience, other sessions) and how much time the briefing takes.

7. What does it cost, and what is included?

Ask for the fee, travel, the recording policy and whether a Q&A or a short follow-up is included. Our general guide on how to choose an AI keynote speaker covers the fee question in more detail.

How do you compare candidates?

Ask every candidate the same seven questions and score the answers on one sheet.

Put the seven questions in one table with a column per candidate and give each answer a score from 1 to 3: 1 for a vague or missing answer, 2 for a usable answer, 3 for a concrete answer with an example or a source. Add two lines for your own observations: a recent clip you watched and a reference you called. The candidate with the highest total is not automatically the right one, but the sheet makes the choice explainable to your board or your programme committee, and it keeps names and reputations out of the first round. We do not publish a ranking of speakers, including ourselves; the scorecard is the neutral route this guide can offer.

European context for the brief

Three recent European sources give the speaker a common starting point with your audience.

  • The Apply AI Strategy, adopted on 8 October 2025 according to the Joint Research Centre, names healthcare and pharma among ten priority sectors and gives AI-powered advanced screening centres as a flagship for the sector.
  • The WHO/Europe assessment of 19 November 2025, drawing on insights from 50 Member States, as the reference for where health systems stand.
  • The AI Act timeline above, with 2 August 2026 as the main date (with some exceptions) and 2 August 2028 for AI in regulated products.

Give the speaker these three, your own figures and the question your audience should be able to answer afterwards.

Frequently asked questions

Should the speaker be a clinician?

That depends on your main audience. Decide first who the keynote is for, then ask each candidate how they have spoken to that audience before.

Can the keynote be online or in English?

Ask each candidate explicitly, and ask how interaction works online.

How do we measure whether the keynote worked?

Agree beforehand on one or two things the audience should start, stop or decide, and ask for it in the evaluation.

To sum up

Choose on audience and goal, check every candidate on the European rules and recent examples, and compare on one scorecard.

A keynote on AI and the future of healthcare works when the audience leaves with a clear picture of what is coming and what to do about it, inside the rules that now apply. Ask the seven questions, score the answers, put the European context in the briefing and measure afterwards.

Choosing a speaker on AI and the future of government for a public sector conference: 7 questions

Richard van Hooijdonk · datum volgt · concept

A speaker on AI and the future of government serves three audiences at once: policymakers, the people who run public services and the citizens who use them. According to the European Commission the EU AI Act became applicable on 2 August 2026, and several of its high-risk examples concern public services. Below: seven questions, one scorecard and the sources.

You are organising a conference for a ministry, a municipality, an agency or an association of public professionals. Everyone expects AI on the programme, but a list of names does not tell you who fits a room of civil servants, directors and elected officials. This guide gives you the questions, a way to compare the answers and the European context to hand over.

Why government is different

Public bodies decide about rights and services, and several of the Commission's high-risk examples under the AI Act are exactly such uses.

Among the high-risk examples on the European Commission's own AI Act page are the automated examination of visa applications, certain AI uses that give access to essential private and public services, and AI solutions that prepare judicial rulings. For several high-risk areas, including migration, asylum and border management, the rules apply from 2 December 2027; the Act itself became applicable on 2 August 2026, with some exceptions, and prohibited practices and AI literacy obligations have applied since 2 February 2025.

The policy direction is set as well. The Apply AI Strategy, adopted on 8 October 2025 according to the Joint Research Centre, makes the public sector a priority next to ten industry sectors, with an AI first policy in which AI is considered as a possible solution for strategic or policy decisions, with attention to benefits and risks, and a buy European approach with emphasis on open source. On 9 April 2026 the Joint Research Centre published a report on advancing AI adoption in EU public administrations, built on three core activities: anchoring AI adoption in EU policies, regulations and principles; adapting the capabilities of public administrations; and applying AI in high-impact domains where it creates public value.

Which 7 questions do you ask?

Seven questions, from the audience and the mandate to the rules, trust and the fee.

1. Who is in the room, and their mandate?

Policy makers, service managers, data teams and elected officials each need a different story. Decide on one main audience for the plenary and ask the speaker how the keynote changes for each group.

2. The AI Act for public bodies?

Ask about the dates above and about the high-risk uses in public services. The Act became applicable on 2 August 2026, with some exceptions; a speaker who presents it as a future problem is behind.

3. Recent public sector examples?

Ask for three concrete cases with the organisation, the year and the source. For background on the topics, see our articles on AI in public services and generative AI in government services.

4. Organisation, not only tools?

Ask how much of the keynote goes to procurement, data sharing between bodies, legislation as code, the role of the civil servant and the trust of citizens, and how much to the technology.

5. Risk, rights and trust?

Bias in decisions about citizens, transparency, cybercrime and the duty to explain belong in the story. Ask how the risks come up and what the audience can do about them.

6. Tailored to your programme and country?

Ask what the speaker needs from you (programme, audience, your own examples) and how the speaker places your country in the European picture.

7. What does it cost, and what is included?

Ask for the fee, travel, the recording policy and whether a Q&A or a short follow-up is included. Our general guide on how to choose an AI keynote speaker covers the fee question in more detail.

How do you compare candidates?

Ask every candidate the same seven questions and score the answers on one sheet.

Put the seven questions in one table with a column per candidate and give each answer a score from 1 to 3: 1 for a vague or missing answer, 2 for a usable answer, 3 for a concrete answer with an example or a source. Add two lines for your own observations: a recent clip you watched and a reference you called. The candidate with the highest total is not automatically the right one, but the sheet makes the choice explainable to your board or your programme committee, and it keeps names and reputations out of the first round. We do not publish a ranking of speakers, including ourselves; the scorecard is the neutral route this guide can offer.

European context for the brief

Three recent sources give the speaker a common starting point with your audience.

  • The AI Act timeline: in force 1 August 2024, applicable 2 August 2026 with some exceptions, prohibited practices and AI literacy since 2 February 2025, several high-risk areas from 2 December 2027.
  • The Apply AI Strategy of 8 October 2025 (date according to the Joint Research Centre): AI first and buy European for the public sector.
  • The JRC report of 9 April 2026 with its three core activities for public administrations.

Add your own figures and the one question your audience should be able to answer afterwards.

Frequently asked questions

Should the speaker come from government?

That depends on your main audience. Decide first who the keynote is for, then ask each candidate how they have spoken to that audience before.

Can the keynote be in English or online?

Ask each candidate explicitly, and ask how interaction works online.

How do we measure whether the keynote worked?

Agree beforehand on one or two things the audience should start, stop or decide, and ask for it in the evaluation.

To sum up

Choose on audience and mandate, check every candidate on the AI Act and recent cases, and compare on one scorecard.

A keynote on AI and the future of government works when civil servants, directors and officials leave with a clear picture of what is coming, what the rules ask and what to do next. Ask the seven questions, score the answers and put the European context in the briefing.

Choosing a keynote speaker on AI for banks and insurers: 7 questions for your financial services event

Richard van Hooijdonk · datum volgt · concept

A keynote speaker on AI for banks and insurers is chosen on what your audience should do differently afterwards. A board, a risk team and a product team each need their own story: according to the European Commission the EU AI Act became applicable on 2 August 2026, and DORA has applied since 17 January 2025.

You are organising a financial services event in Europe, perhaps for the boards of banks and insurers, an industry association, a pension provider or a technology supplier. Everyone wants AI on the programme, but a list of speaker names does not tell you who fits your room. This guide gives you the questions, a way to compare the answers and the facts for the briefing.

Why a finance specialist?

Banks and insurers work under supervisors and standard setters who have already said what they expect from AI.

For the 196 UK firms that answered the 2026 survey by the Bank of England and the FCA, AI is no longer an experiment: 86% of respondents reported using AI, up from 75% in 2024, and 80% reported having AI agents deployed. Most respondents keep those agents on a short leash: 80% reported agents that provide decision support, where the agent recommends and humans decide and execute, and only 2% reported agents with broad autonomy. That is the kind of detail a general AI talk misses and a financial audience notices.

Supervisors and standard setters have spoken too. The Financial Stability Board named four AI-related vulnerabilities in November 2024, starting with third-party dependencies and service provider concentration, and in October 2026 it published 12 sound practices as recommendations that it strongly encourages boards and senior management to reference. The European Central Bank wrote in its Financial Stability Review of May 2024 that widespread AI adoption with concentrated suppliers may increase operational risk, market concentration and herding behaviour. A speaker who knows that landscape can place your organisation in it, instead of giving a generic AI talk.

Which 7 questions do you ask?

Seven questions, from the audience and the goal to the rules and the fee.

1. Who is in the room, what should change?

Decide on one main audience: the board, the risk and compliance teams, the product and IT teams, or the advisers and account managers who talk to customers. Ask the speaker how the story changes for each of them, and what the audience should start, stop or decide after the session.

2. Does the speaker know the European rules?

Ask when the AI Act applies to your use cases. The Commission's own page says the Act entered into force on 1 August 2024 and became applicable on 2 August 2026, with some exceptions; prohibited practices and AI literacy obligations have applied since 2 February 2025. Credit scoring that denies citizens the opportunity to obtain a loan is one of the high-risk examples on that page, and the rules for high-risk systems under Annex III, which include that use case, apply from 2 December 2027 as a result of the AI Omnibus, which entered into force on 27 July 2026. Ask about DORA as well: according to the European Banking Authority it became applicable on 17 January 2025, and financial entities in scope need a register of their contractual arrangements with ICT third-party service providers. For insurers, EIOPA's opinion of 6 August 2025 clarifies the key principles and requirements in insurance-sector legislation for the use and supervision of AI systems; for investment services, ESMA expects firms to comply with the relevant MiFID II requirements, including the obligation to act in the best interest of the client. A speaker who gets these dates and names wrong is likely to get the rest wrong too.

3. Recent banking and insurance examples?

Ask for three concrete examples with the organisation, the year and the source. Ask as well whether the speaker has commercial ties to AI vendors or to the organisations in the examples, and whether those ties are mentioned on stage; for a programme committee that can be the deciding factor. For background on the topics, see our articles on AI agents in financial services and how generative AI is reshaping the finance sector.

4. Organisation as well as technology?

Ask how much of the keynote goes to work processes, model risk, accountability and people, and how much to the technology itself. The FSB's recommended practices emphasise organisation-wide AI governance and risk management throughout the AI lifecycle; a keynote that only shows tools leaves the board with nothing to decide.

5. Risk, concentration and trust?

Bias in credit and pricing decisions, errors, liability, cyber risk and customer trust belong in the story. So does concentration: in the Bank of England and FCA survey, the top three cloud and compute providers accounted for 79% of all reported relationships and the top three model and API providers for 54%. Ask how these risks come up in the keynote and what the audience can do about them.

6. Is the keynote tailored to your programme?

Ask what the speaker needs from you (programme, audience, other sessions, the products or markets you want covered) and how much time the briefing takes.

7. What does it cost, and what is included?

Ask for the fee, travel, the recording policy and whether a Q&A or a short follow-up is included. Our general guide on how to choose an AI keynote speaker covers the fee question in more detail.

How do you compare candidates?

Ask every candidate the same seven questions and score the answers on one sheet.

Give each answer a score from 1 to 3: 1 for a vague or missing answer, 2 for a usable answer, 3 for a concrete answer with an example or a source. Add two lines for your own observations, scored on the same scale: a recent clip you watched (1 weak, 2 usable, 3 convincing) and a reference you called (1 for doubts, 2 for a neutral account, 3 for a clear recommendation). Both count in the total, so the maximum is 27 points. You can copy the table below.

| Question | Candidate A | Candidate B | Candidate C | |---|---|---|---| | 1. Audience and what they should do differently | | | | | 2. European rules: AI Act, DORA, sector supervisors | | | | | 3. Recent examples with sources, commercial ties stated | | | | | 4. Organisation, not only technology | | | | | 5. Risk, concentration and trust | | | | | 6. Tailored to your programme | | | | | 7. Fee and what is included | | | | | Recent clip watched (1 to 3) | | | | | Reference called (1 to 3) | | | | | Total | | | |

The candidate with the highest total is not automatically the right one, but the sheet makes the choice explainable to your board or your programme committee, and it keeps names and reputations out of the first round. We do not publish a ranking of speakers, including ourselves; the scorecard is the neutral route this guide can offer.

Context for the briefing

Four recent sources, two European, one international and one British, give the speaker a common starting point with your audience.

  • The AI Act timeline above, with 2 August 2026 as the main date (with some exceptions) and 2 December 2027 for high-risk systems under Annex III, such as credit scoring.
  • DORA, applicable since 17 January 2025, and the 12 sound practices of the FSB of October 2026, recommendations on governance and risk management throughout the AI lifecycle.
  • The 2026 UK survey by the Bank of England and the FCA, with 196 responding UK firms, as a benchmark from one market for where firms stand with AI and AI agents; it is not an EU figure.

Give the speaker these four, your own figures and the question your audience should be able to answer afterwards.

Frequently asked questions

Must the speaker come from finance?

That depends on your main audience. Decide first who the keynote is for, then ask each candidate how they have spoken to that audience before and which examples from the sector they bring.

Can the keynote be online or in English?

Ask each candidate explicitly, and ask how interaction works online.

How do we measure whether the keynote worked?

Agree beforehand on one or two things the audience should start, stop or decide, and ask for it in the evaluation.

To sum up

Choose on audience and goal, check every candidate on the European rules and recent examples, and compare on one scorecard.

A keynote on AI for banks and insurers works when the audience leaves with a clear picture of what is coming and what to do about it, inside the rules that now apply. Ask the seven questions, score the answers, put the European context in the briefing and measure afterwards.

Choosing a futurist keynote speaker on AI in logistics and supply chains: 7 questions for your logistics summit

Richard van Hooijdonk · datum volgt · concept

A futurist keynote on AI in logistics is chosen on what your audience should do differently afterwards, not on a name. Shippers, carriers and ports each need their own story: according to the European Commission the EU AI Act became applicable on 2 August 2026. Below: seven questions, one scorecard and the sources.

You are organising a logistics summit, perhaps for an industry association, a port, a group of shippers and carriers or a technology supplier. Everyone wants AI on the programme, but a list of speaker names does not tell you who fits your room. This guide does not name a speaker, including the author of this blog; it gives you the questions, a way to compare the answers and the facts for the briefing, so that the choice is yours and explainable.

Why a logistics specialist?

Logistics spans five transport modes and many parties, and the European rules are changing on a fixed timetable.

Among the EU enterprises that use AI, logistics is the least common application. According to Eurostat, 19.95% of EU enterprises used AI technologies in 2025, and AI software or systems for logistics were, with 6.08% of the enterprises using AI, the least used type. That figure covers AI-using enterprises in all sectors, not the adoption of AI by the logistics sector itself, so ask the speaker what they know about your part of the sector and what the first useful steps are.

The sector is also more than trucks. Eurostat measures freight transport performance in tonne-kilometres across five modes; by that measure, maritime transport accounted for 67.0% in the EU in 2024, road for 25.7%, rail for 5.4%, inland waterways for 1.7% and air for 0.2%. Your audience may sit in one of those modes; ask the speaker which examples cross modes and which do not. And the European Commission is digitalising the paperwork: under the eFTI Regulation, authorities in all EU Member States will be required to accept freight transport information shared electronically via certified eFTI platforms, in road, rail, inland waterway and air transport, and the Regulation will apply in full on 9 July 2027. CountEmissionsEU entered into force on 1 June 2026 as the first EU-wide standardised methodology for calculating greenhouse-gas emissions from freight and passenger transport, aligned with EN ISO 14083:2023; it applies to EU companies that voluntarily disclose transport emissions. Do electronic freight data and comparable emissions data change what AI can do for your audience? Put that question to the speaker. A speaker who knows that landscape can place your organisation in it, instead of giving a generic AI talk.

Which 7 questions do you ask?

Seven questions, from the audience and the goal to the rules and the fee.

1. Who is in the room, what should change?

Decide on one main audience: the boards of shippers and carriers, the planners and operations managers, the IT and data teams, or the port and terminal operators. Ask the speaker how the story changes for each of them, and what the audience should start, stop or decide after the session.

2. Does the speaker know the European rules?

Ask when the AI Act applies to your use cases. The Commission's own page says the Act entered into force on 1 August 2024 and became applicable on 2 August 2026, with some exceptions; prohibited practices and AI literacy obligations have applied since 2 February 2025. Two high-risk examples on that page matter for logistics: AI safety components in critical infrastructures, with transport as the example, and AI tools for employment and the management of workers. Ask the speaker whether, and how, that second category applies to the planning tools your audience uses for drivers and warehouse staff; the Commission's page does not spell that out. The rules for high-risk systems under Annex III apply from 2 December 2027 as a result of the AI Omnibus, which entered into force on 27 July 2026. Ask about the eFTI Regulation as well, with 9 July 2027 as the date of full application, and about CountEmissionsEU, in force since 1 June 2026. A speaker who gets these dates wrong is likely to get the rest wrong too.

3. Recent logistics examples, with sources?

Ask for three concrete examples with the organisation, the year and the source. Ask as well whether the speaker has commercial ties to AI vendors, platforms or the organisations in the examples, and whether those ties are mentioned on stage; for a programme committee that can be the deciding factor. For background on the topics, see our articles on AI in logistics: a six-step roadmap from pilot to practice and emerging technologies shaping the future of logistics.

4. Organisation as well as technology?

Ask how much of the keynote goes to planners, drivers and warehouse staff, to data agreements between the parties in the chain and to accountability, and how much to the technology itself. A keynote that only shows tools leaves the board with nothing to decide.

5. How does the speaker treat risk and trust?

Dependence on a few platforms and data providers, errors in automated planning, cyber risk, the position of workers and the trust of customers belong in the story. Ask how these risks come up in the keynote and what the audience can do about them.

6. Is the keynote tailored to your programme?

Ask what the speaker needs from you (programme, audience, other sessions, the modes and markets you want covered) and how much time the briefing takes.

7. What does it cost, and what is included?

Ask for the fee, travel, the recording policy and whether a Q&A or a short follow-up is included. Our general guide on how to choose an AI keynote speaker covers the fee question in more detail.

How do you compare candidates?

Ask every candidate the same seven questions and score the answers on one sheet.

Give each answer a score from 1 to 3: 1 for a vague or missing answer, 2 for a usable answer, 3 for a concrete answer with an example or a source. Add two lines for your own observations, scored on the same scale: a recent clip you watched (1 weak, 2 usable, 3 convincing) and a reference you called (1 for doubts, 2 for a neutral account, 3 for a clear recommendation). Both count in the total, so the maximum is 27 points. You can copy the table below.

| Question | Candidate A | Candidate B | Candidate C | |---|---|---|---| | 1. Audience and what they should do differently | | | | | 2. European rules: AI Act, eFTI, CountEmissionsEU | | | | | 3. Recent examples with sources, commercial ties stated | | | | | 4. Organisation, not only technology | | | | | 5. Risk and trust | | | | | 6. Tailored to your programme | | | | | 7. Fee and what is included | | | | | Recent clip watched (1 to 3) | | | | | Reference called (1 to 3) | | | | | Total | | | |

The candidate with the highest total is not automatically the right one, but the sheet makes the choice explainable to your board or your programme committee, and it keeps names and reputations out of the first round. We do not publish a ranking of speakers, including ourselves; the scorecard is the neutral route this guide can offer.

European context for the brief

Four topics from recent European sources give the speaker a common starting point with your audience.

  • The AI Act timeline above, with 2 August 2026 as the main date (with some exceptions) and 2 December 2027 for high-risk systems under Annex III, such as safety components in transport infrastructure and tools for the management of workers.
  • The eFTI Regulation, with full application on 9 July 2027, and CountEmissionsEU, in force since 1 June 2026, as the two pieces of European digitalisation the audience will hear about; CountEmissionsEU applies to companies that voluntarily disclose transport emissions.
  • The Apply AI Strategy, which names mobility, transport and automotive among the industrial sectors it covers.
  • Eurostat's figures on AI use in enterprises and the modal split of freight transport, as context on how much AI is used and how freight is distributed over the five modes; they say nothing about AI in the logistics sector itself.

Give the speaker these four topics, your own figures and the question your audience should be able to answer afterwards.

Frequently asked questions

Should the speaker come from logistics?

That depends on your main audience. Decide first who the keynote is for, then ask each candidate how they have spoken to that audience before and which examples from the sector they bring.

What does a futurist add to a summit?

A futurist looks further ahead than the next budget year and connects AI to the other changes in the sector, such as electronic freight data, emissions accounting and the labour market; our article on what a futurist or trend watcher does explains the role. Ask each candidate how far ahead they look and which sources they base that outlook on.

Can the keynote be online or in English?

Ask each candidate explicitly, and ask how interaction works online.

How do we measure whether the keynote worked?

Agree beforehand on one or two things the audience should start, stop or decide, and ask for it in the evaluation.

To sum up

Choose on audience and goal, check every candidate on the European rules and recent examples, and compare on one scorecard.

A futurist keynote on AI in logistics and supply chains works when the audience leaves with a clear picture of what is coming and what to do about it, inside the rules that now apply. Ask the seven questions, score the answers, put the European context in the briefing and measure afterwards.
